I’ll make excuses not to go near certain panels or breakers, or I’ll try to get put on something else if a job site feels too much like that one. I mean… I wouldn’t want to say I can’t function at all. I still get up, I still go to work, I try to do what I’m supposed to do.

Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about the sound of a breaker snapping or a tool dropping that triggers your jumpiness, or could it be any loud noise?\n\nPatient: I think it’s mostly those sharp, sudden noises… like a breaker snapping or a tool hitting the floor. Not every loud noise, exactly, but anything that catches me off guard like that. Or if somebody comes up behind me and I don’t know they’re there, I just jump like crazy. I try not to make it a whole thing, but my heart keeps pounding way longer than it probably should.\n\nDoctor: Has anything particularly stressful or traumatic happened to you recently or in the past that might be connected to your jumpiness?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess there is. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but there was an arc-flash accident on a retrofit job. That was, uh… about a year and a month ago now. The jumpiness has pretty much been going on since then, as far as I can tell.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain situations or places that remind you of the arc-flash accident?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I try not to, but I guess I do. I’ll make excuses not to go near certain panels or breakers, or I’ll try to get put on something else if a job site feels too much like that one. And I don’t really like talking about that day either. Once it starts playing in my head, I have a hard time shutting it off. I know that probably sounds like I’m being difficult or something, but I’m not trying to be.\n\nDoctor: Do you have memories or images of the arc-flash accident that come into your mind when you don't want them to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes. I mean, I try not to let it get to me, but it’ll just pop in there out of nowhere. Like I’ll be doing something normal, loading tools in the truck or whatever, and I’ll see that flash in my head, or hear the trainee yelling.\n\nIt kind of knocks me sideways for a second, like I’m right back there. I know that sounds… I don’t know, maybe over the top, but that’s what it feels like.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid talking about the arc-flash accident with other people?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Not all the time, I mean, I’m not trying to be rude about it, but if somebody brings it up I usually try to change the subject or keep it short. I’ll make some excuse, like I’ve got something else to do.\n\nIt’s just… once I start talking about that day, it gets going in my head and I can’t really shut it off. So I try not to go there, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're constantly on the lookout for potential dangers or threats, even when you're not in a situation that would normally be considered dangerous?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure… I don’t think of it like I’m looking for danger all the time. Maybe I’m a little more aware of what’s around me than I used to be, but I don’t know if that’s what you mean.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. I guess I’d have to think about it more.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself always being on the lookout for something that might go wrong, even when things seem safe?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… “always” sounds pretty strong. I wouldn’t say I’m always doing that, no. I mean, I like to know what’s going on around me, especially at work, but I think most guys would, right?\n\nMaybe I’m a little quicker to notice stuff now, but I haven’t really thought of it as being on the lookout all the time. Does that answer it okay?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're on edge or extremely alert, even when there's no apparent reason to be?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it “extremely” alert. I mean… I do get tense sometimes, but I don’t know if it’s for no reason exactly.\n\nI guess I haven’t really paid attention to whether I’m on edge when nothing’s going on. I don’t want to overstate it. Maybe there are times, but… I’m not sure how to answer that the right way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're waiting for something bad to happen, even when everything seems okay?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I don’t think I sit there expecting something bad exactly. Maybe I get a little uneasy sometimes, but “waiting for something bad to happen” sounds kind of strong.\n\nI guess if everything seems okay, I try to just tell myself it’s fine. I’m not sure if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're watching for potential threats or dangers, even when you're in a safe environment?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I notice exits and people coming in and out sometimes, but I wouldn’t say I’m “watching for threats” exactly. That makes it sound kind of intense.\n\nMaybe I’m just trying to be aware, you know? I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. In places that are safe, I think I’m mostly okay… at least I think so. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding people, places, or activities because they remind you of the arc-flash accident?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do, more than I probably want to admit.\n\nI’ll make excuses sometimes so I don’t have to go near certain panels or breakers, or certain job sites if they remind me of it. I try to make it sound like it’s scheduling or I’ve got something else to handle, but… it’s really because once I start thinking about that day, it gets going in my head and I can’t shut it off.\n\nI don’t like talking about it much either. I’m not trying to be difficult, it just… yeah, I avoid it when I can.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think that you're to blame for the arc-flash accident or that it's had a lasting negative impact on who you are as a person?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I try not to think about it like that, because I know that probably doesn’t help anything.\n\nBut, yeah. I do feel guilty. A lot. Like I failed that kid somehow, even if people tell me it wasn’t all on me. I walk around mad at myself and guilty most of the time, and it feels like everybody can see it on me, like it’s written all over my face.\n\nI don’t know if that’s what you’re asking, but… it’s definitely changed how I see myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think that you're a bad person because of what happened during the arc-flash accident?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “bad person” exactly… I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I’m sitting there saying that to myself all day.\n\nBut I do walk around mad at myself. Guilty. Like I failed that kid somehow. And it feels like people can see it on me, like they know. So… maybe it’s not “bad person” in those words, but it feels pretty close sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think that you're worthless or that your life has no meaning because of what happened during the arc-flash accident?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d put it as “worthless,” exactly… I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nBut I do walk around mad at myself and guilty most of the time. Like I failed that kid, and like everybody can see that on me somehow. So… it gets pretty hard to feel good about myself, I guess. If that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think that you're a failure because of what happened during the arc-flash accident?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, “failure” is a hard word, but… yeah, it feels that way a lot.\n\nI walk around mad at myself and guilty, like I failed that kid somehow. And then I feel like everybody can see it on me, like they can tell. I know that probably doesn’t make sense, but that’s kind of how it sits with me most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think that you're unable to function as a person because of what happened during the arc-flash accident?\n\nPatient: I mean… I wouldn’t want to say I can’t function at all. I still get up, I still go to work, I try to do what I’m supposed to do.\n\nBut… yeah, it’s been affecting things more than I like to admit. I’ve been written up at work, and they took me out of consideration for supervisor. And at home my wife says I’m barely there, like I’m present but not really with them.\n\nA lot of my day is just… trying to make sure nothing happens like that again, trying to avoid another blowup. So I don’t know if that counts as “unable to function,” but it’s definitely not how I used to be.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
